```text
3	
4	When we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (MH-DIF) analysis~\citep{holland1988mh} with Educational Testing Service (ETS) severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\citep{kipnis2024metabench},\footnote{Analysis code and DIF audit scripts are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/dif-audit}.} and find that 31\% of MMLU~\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$), indicating large shifts in item functioning between models released in 2023 and 2024. This is not statistical noise: a random-split control within the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\% ETS~C items. This pattern persists across all 57 MMLU subjects (C\% range 21--48\%, unweighted mean $\approx 30\%$), and item-level regression shows that 92.4\% of the DIF signal is unrelated to aggregate domain-level accuracy gains. Nearly one in three MMLU items behaves differently depending on when the evaluated models were trained. Crucially, this measurement instability does not threaten benchmark validity: Spearman rank correlation between model rankings computed on all items versus stable-only items is $\rho \approx 0.997$. The instability is diagnostic, not destructive; it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations rather than rendering MMLU scores unreliable.
5	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01227M7CTenPUBmFuiMMNsJV`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Method Details}
2	\label{app:method_details}
3	
4	\subsection{Diagnostics}
5	\label{app:diagnostics}
6	
7	Three diagnostic procedures complement the primary DIF analysis.
8	
9	\paragraph{Local independence (Yen's Q3).} The MH procedure assumes that item responses are conditionally independent given $\theta$. Yen's Q3 statistic measures residual correlations between item pairs after conditioning on the latent trait. We compute Q3 for all within-subject and cross-subject item pairs, with $|Q3| > 0.20$ as the threshold for local dependence. Elevated within-subject dependence would indicate that items within the same subject share variance beyond what $\theta$ captures, which could inflate within-subject DIF rates.
10	
11	\paragraph{Domain-specificity.} We test whether temporal DIF concentrates in domains where models improved most. At the ecological level, we compute Spearman $\rho$ between subject-level C\% and subject-level accuracy improvement from 2023 to 2024. At the item level, logistic regression predicts item-level DIF~C classification from domain-level accuracy change, with and without item property controls (difficulty, discrimination). This decomposition quantifies how much of the observed instability is attributable to aggregate domain-level gains versus item-specific behavioral change.
12	
13	\paragraph{Instability decomposition.} When domain accuracy improvement is included alongside item-level controls, we compute the counterfactual C\% that would remain after removing domain-level effects. The gap between observed and counterfactual C\% measures the contribution of domain-level improvement to the total instability.
14	
15	\subsection{Statistical Considerations}
16	\label{app:statistical}
17	
18	\paragraph{Multiple testing.} The ETS~C classification imposes a dual threshold (effect size $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$ and significance $p < 0.05$), which provides implicit control over false positives. As a robustness check, we apply Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction at $q = 0.05$. The random-split sanity check provides an empirical estimate of the false positive rate under the null hypothesis.
19	
20	\paragraph{Threshold sensitivity.} We report results across a range of DIF thresholds ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \in \{1.0, 1.5, 2.0, 2.5\}$) to verify that qualitative conclusions do not depend on the specific ETS~C cutoff. The standard threshold of $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$ corresponds to the ETS~C definition used throughout the paper.
21	
22	\paragraph{IRT model fit.} For transparency, we report that the MMLU response matrix shows poor fit to a unidimensional 2PL model (expected for a 57-subject multidimensional benchmark). This does not affect MH-DIF validity, which relies only on total score stratification rather than parametric IRT assumptions.
23	
24	\paragraph{Architecture heterogeneity.} The METABENCH MMLU population consists of 99.98\% decoder-only models (5,226 of 5,227). This near-complete architectural homogeneity eliminates architecture as a confound for DIF analysis. We verify this by excluding the single encoder-decoder model and confirming identical results.
25	
26	\paragraph{Power sensitivity to item discrimination.}
27	\label{app:power_sensitivity}
28	The Monte Carlo power analysis in \Cref{sec:validation} uses average 2PL parameters ($\bar{a} = 1.2$, $\bar{b} = -0.84$). \Cref{tab:power_sensitivity} reports TPR as a function of discrimination $a$ (other parameters at their means, $N = 80$ per cohort, $|\Delta_b| = 1.4$, 1,000 replications). TPR increases monotonically from 0.40 ($a = 0.5$) to 0.95 ($a = 2.0$); FPR remains below 0.04 throughout. The MMLU item pool shows substantial heterogeneity: $a$ ranges from 0.006 to 2.657 (SD $= 0.54$, IQR $= [0.75, 1.64]$). The aggregate TPR of 0.68 from Phase~2 reflects this full distribution, while TPR at mean $a \approx 1.2$ is 0.87---the gap is consistent with Jensen's inequality applied to the concave TPR($a$) function.
29	
30	\begin{table}[h]
31	\centering
32	\caption{Power sensitivity to item discrimination. TPR = true positive rate for ETS~C detection ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$) at $N = 80$ per cohort, $|\Delta_b| = 1.4$, 1,000 replications.}
33	\label{tab:power_sensitivity}
34	\small
35	\begin{tabular}{rccc}
36	\toprule
37	$a$ & TPR & 95\% CI & FPR \\
38	\midrule
39	0.5 & 0.398 & [0.20, 0.60] & 0.033 \\
40	0.8 & 0.683 & [0.45, 0.90] & 0.036 \\
41	1.0 & 0.808 & [0.60, 0.95] & 0.038 \\
42	1.2 ($\approx \bar{a}$) & 0.874 & [0.70, 1.00] & 0.038 \\
43	1.5 & 0.930 & [0.80, 1.00] & 0.038 \\
44	2.0 & 0.949 & [0.80, 1.00] & 0.037 \\
45	\bottomrule
46	\end{tabular}
47	\end{table}
48	
49	\paragraph{Logistic regression specification.} The Simpson's paradox analysis (\Cref{sec:temporal}) uses binary logistic regression with DIF-C classification as the dependent variable, domain-level accuracy change (2024 minus 2023 mean accuracy) as the primary predictor, and item difficulty (proportion correct) and IRT discrimination (point-biserial correlation) as controls. The interaction between domain accuracy change and item difficulty is included. Non-uniform DIF detection (\Cref{sec:structural}) uses logistic regression with a group $\times$ ability interaction term; a significant interaction ($p < 0.05$) indicates non-uniform DIF.
50	
51	\section{Structural Analysis Details}
52	\label{app:structural}
53	
54	Three progressively refined approaches fail to reduce the temporal false positive rate below 31\%, revealing a structural property of the data rather than a methodological limitation. Matched-ability quintile analysis restricts comparisons to models of similar total score and yields enrichment ratios of 0.94--1.04, with temporal FPR unchanged at approximately 0.31. IRT $\theta$-conditioning with 20 ability strata achieves $r(\theta, \mathrm{total\ score}) = 0.9986$ and FPR $= 0.312$, confirming that the external total score already captures nearly all ability variance relevant to DIF computation. Within-subject DIF analysis on 5 representative subjects, intended to isolate subject-specific effects, finds mean C\% $= 38.1\%$, exceeding the 25\% threshold that would indicate viable bifactor structure. \Cref{tab:structural_comparison} summarizes these results.
55	
56	The temporal FPR reflects genuine item-level instability that persists regardless of how ability is measured or conditioned. Yen's Q3 diagnostic reveals mild local dependence within subjects (11.3\% of within-subject item pairs exceed $|Q3| > 0.20$, vs.\ 2.2\% cross-subject; $5{\times}$ ratio, $p = 2.3 \times 10^{-9}$), which contributes to elevated within-subject DIF rates but is not the primary driver: the cross-subject matching protocol addresses this bias, and temporal C\% persists under external matching (full Q3 distributions in Appendix~Figure~A2).
57

Cross-subject matching eliminates FPR inflation ($\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$) while preserving detection power ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ for 4/5 subjects at $p = 0.5$). Within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average.}

Yen's Q3 statistic measures residual correlations between item pairs after conditioning on the latent trait. We compute Q3 for all within-subject and cross-subject item pairs, with $|Q3| > 0.20$ as the threshold for local dependence. Elevated within-subject dependence would indicate that items within the same subject share variance beyond what $\\theta$ captures, which could inflate within-subject DIF rates.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain-specificity.} We test whether temporal DIF concentrates in domains where models improved most.     "originalFile": "\\section{Method Details}\n\\label{app:method_details}\n\n\\subsection{Diagnostics}\n\\label{app:diagnostics}\n\nThree diagnostic procedures complement the primary DIF analysis.\n\n\\paragraph{Local independence (Yen's Q3).} The MH procedure assumes that item responses are conditionally independent given $\\theta$. Yen's Q3 statistic measures residual correlations between item pairs after conditioning on the latent trait. We compute Q3 for all within-subject and cross-subject item pairs, with $|Q3| > 0.20$ as the threshold for local dependence. Elevated within-subject dependence would indicate that items within the same subject share variance beyond what $\\theta$ captures, which could inflate within-subject DIF rates.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain-specificity.} We test whether temporal DIF concentrates in domains where models improved most. At the ecological level, we compute Spearman $\\rho$ between subject-level C\\% and subject-level accuracy improvement from 2023 to 2024. At the item level, logistic regression predicts item-level DIF~C classification from domain-level accuracy change, with and without item property controls (difficulty, discrimination). This decomposition quantifies how much of the observed instability is attributable to aggregate domain-level gains versus item-specific behavioral change.\n\n\\paragraph{Instability decomposition.} When domain accuracy improvement is included alongside item-level controls, we compute the counterfactual C\\% that would remain after removing domain-level effects. The gap between observed and counterfactual C\\% measures the contribution of domain-level improvement to the total instability.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Considerations}\n\\label{app:statistical}\n\n\\paragraph{Multiple testing.} The ETS~C classification imposes a dual threshold (effect size $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ and significance $p < 0.05$), which provides implicit control over false positives. As a robustness check, we apply Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction at $q = 0.05$. The random-split sanity check provides an empirical estimate of the false positive rate under the null hypothesis.\n\n\\paragraph{Threshold sensitivity.} We report results across a range of DIF thresholds ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\in \\{1.0, 1.5, 2.0, 2.5\\}$) to verify that qualitative conclusions do not depend on the specific ETS~C cutoff. The standard threshold of $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ corresponds to the ETS~C definition used throughout the paper.\n\n\\paragraph{IRT model fit.} For transparency, we report that the MMLU response matrix shows poor fit to a unidimensional 2PL model (expected for a 57-subject multidimensional benchmark). This does not affect MH-DIF validity, which relies only on total score stratification rather than parametric IRT assumptions.\n\n\\paragraph{Architecture heterogeneity.} The METABENCH MMLU population consists of 99.98\\% decoder-only models (5,226 of 5,227). This near-complete architectural homogeneity eliminates architecture as a confound for DIF analysis. We verify this by excluding the single encoder-decoder model and confirming identical results.\n\n\\paragraph{Power sensitivity to item discrimination.}\n\\label{app:power_sensitivity}\nThe Monte Carlo power analysis in \\Cref{sec:validation} uses average 2PL parameters ($\\bar{a} = 1.2$, $\\bar{b} = -0.84$). \\Cref{tab:power_sensitivity} reports TPR as a function of discrimination $a$ (other parameters at their means, $N = 80$ per cohort, $|\\Delta_b| = 1.4$, 1,000 replications). TPR increases monotonically from 0.40 ($a = 0.5$) to 0.95 ($a = 2.0$); FPR remains below 0.04 throughout. The MMLU item pool shows substantial heterogeneity: $a$ ranges from 0.006 to 2.657 (SD $= 0.54$, IQR $= [0.75, 1.64]$). The aggregate TPR of 0.68 from Phase~2 reflects this full distribution, while TPR at mean $a \\approx 1.2$ is 0.87---the gap is consistent with Jensen's inequality applied to the concave TPR($a$) function.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Power sensitivity to item discrimination. TPR = true positive rate for ETS~C detection ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$) at $N = 80$ per cohort, $|\\Delta_b| = 1.4$, 1,000 replications.}\n\\label{tab:power_sensitivity}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rccc}\n\\toprule\n$a$ & TPR & 95\\% CI & FPR \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.5 & 0.398 & [0.20, 0.60] & 0.033 \\\\\n0.8 & 0.683 & [0.45, 0.90] & 0.036 \\\\\n1.0 & 0.808 & [0.60, 0.95] & 0.038 \\\\\n1.2 ($\\approx \\bar{a}$) & 0.874 & [0.70, 1.00] & 0.038 \\\\\n1.5 & 0.930 & [0.80, 1.00] & 0.038 \\\\\n2.0 & 0.949 & [0.80, 1.00] & 0.037 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Logistic regression specification.} The Simpson's paradox analysis (\\Cref{sec:temporal}) uses binary logistic regression with DIF-C classification as the dependent variable, domain-level accuracy change (2024 minus 2023 mean accuracy) as the primary predictor, and item difficulty (proportion correct) and IRT discrimination (point-biserial correlation) as controls. The interaction between domain accuracy change and item difficulty is included. Non-uniform DIF detection (\\Cref{sec:structural}) uses logistic regression with a group $\\times$ ability interaction term; a significant interaction ($p < 0.05$) indicates non-uniform DIF.\n\n\\section{Structural Analysis Details}\n\\label{app:structural}\n\nThree progressively refined approaches fail to reduce the temporal false positive rate below 31\\%, revealing a structural property of the data rather than a methodological limitation. Matched-ability quintile analysis restricts comparisons to models of similar total score and yields enrichment ratios of 0.94--1.04, with temporal FPR unchanged at approximately 0.31. IRT $\\theta$-conditioning with 20 ability strata achieves $r(\\theta, \\mathrm{total\\ score}) = 0.9986$ and FPR $= 0.312$, confirming that the external total score already captures nearly all ability variance relevant to DIF computation. Within-subject DIF analysis on 5 representative subjects, intended to isolate subject-specific effects, finds mean C\\% $= 38.1\\%$, exceeding the 25\\% threshold that would indicate viable bifactor structure. \\Cref{tab:structural_comparison} summarizes these results.\n\nThe temporal FPR reflects genuine item-level instability that persists regardless of how ability is measured or conditioned. Yen's Q3 diagnostic reveals mild local dependence within subjects (11.3\\% of within-subject item pairs exceed $|Q3| > 0.20$, vs.\\ 2.2\\% cross-subject; $5{\\times}$ ratio, $p = 2.3 \\times 10^{-9}$), which contributes to elevated within-subject DIF rates but is not the primary driver: the cross-subject matching protocol addresses this bias, and temporal C\\% persists under external matching (full Q3 distributions in Appendix~Figure~A2).\n\n\\section{Additional Figures}\n\\label{app:figures}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_3_within_subject_matching.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-subject vs.\\ within-subject matching for semi-synthetic DIF detection. Cross-subject matching eliminates FPR inflation ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$) while preserving detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ for 4/5 subjects at $p = 0.5$). Within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average.}\n\\label{app:fig_matching}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_4_downstream_impact.pdf}\n\\caption{Downstream impact of DIF-based item filtering on model rankings. Despite $\\rho \\approx 0.997$ overall, individual model families show systematic rank shifts when evaluated on stable items only.}\n\\label{app:fig_downstream}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_5_gsm1k_dif_correlation.pdf}\n\\caption{GSM1K contamination gap vs.\\ DIF ranking advantage ($N = 33$). The negative correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$) indicates that models with larger contamination gaps benefit less from DIF-flagged items.}\n\\label{app:fig_gsm1k}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Multi-subject injection robustness. $k$ = number of simultaneously contaminated subjects (out of 57); values are mean $\\pm$ SD across 5 random subject draws at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$.}\n\\label{tab:multi_subject_injection}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rcc}\n\\toprule\n$k$ & $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ & $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 & $0.000_{\\pm.000}$ & $0.371_{\\pm.048}$ \\\\\n3 & $0.003_{\\pm.002}$ & $0.385_{\\pm.031}$ \\\\\n5 & $0.007_{\\pm.003}$ & $0.392_{\\pm.027}$ \\\\\n10 & $0.047_{\\pm.022}$ & $0.401_{\\pm.024}$ \\\\\n20 & $0.069_{\\pm.018}$ & $0.410_{\\pm.020}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Cross-Benchmark Comparison}\n\\label{app:gsm8k_details}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-benchmark comparison of DIF analysis on MMLU and GSM8K. Both benchmarks exhibit temporal measurement instability with validated semi-synthetic detection power. Standard and external matching use different exploitation rates ($p$) because each benchmark's optimal operating point differs; external matching results are compared at $p = 0.5$ for both. Subscript $\\pm$ values denote standard deviations across 5 random injection seeds.}\n\\label{tab:gsm8k_comparison}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nMetric & MMLU & GSM8K \\\\\n\\midrule\nItems & 12,508 & 1,319 \\\\\nModels & 5,227 & 6,702 \\\\\nTemporal C\\% & 31.3\\% & 18.9\\% \\\\\nSanity C\\% & 0.18\\% & 0.3\\% \\\\\n$\\Delta$TPR ($p{=}0.7$, standard) & -- & $+0.818_{\\pm.006}$ \\\\\n$\\Delta$TPR ($p{=}0.5$, external) & $+0.508_{\\pm.027}$ & $+0.514_{\\pm.028}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Method Details}\n\\label{app:method_details}\n\n\\subsection{Diagnostics}\n\\label{app:diagnostics}\n\nThree diagnostic procedures complement the primary DIF analysis.\n\n\\paragraph{Local independence (Yen's Q3).} The MH procedure assumes that item responses are conditionally independent given $\\theta$. Yen's Q3 statistic measures residual correlations between item pairs after conditioning on the latent trait. We compute Q3 for all within-subject and cross-subject item pairs, with $|Q3| > 0.20$ as the threshold for local dependence. Elevated within-subject dependence would indicate that items within the same subject share variance beyond what $\\theta$ captures, which could inflate within-subject DIF rates.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain-specificity.} We test whether temporal DIF concentrates in domains where models improved most. At the ecological level, we compute Spearman $\\rho$ between subject-level C\\% and subject-level accuracy improvement from 2023 to 2024. At the item level, logistic regression predicts item-level DIF~C classification from domain-level accuracy change, with and without item property controls (difficulty, discrimination). This decomposition quantifies how much of the observed instability is attributable to aggregate domain-level gains versus item-specific behavioral change.\n\n\\paragraph{Instability decomposition.} When domain accuracy improvement is included alongside item-level controls, we compute the counterfactual C\\% that would remain after removing domain-level effects. The gap between observed and counterfactual C\\% measures the contribution of domain-level improvement to the total instability.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Considerations}\n\\label{app:statistical}\n\n\\paragraph{Multiple testing.} The ETS~C classification imposes a dual threshold (effect size $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ and significance $p < 0.05$), which provides implicit control over false positives. As a robustness check, we apply Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction at $q = 0.05$. The random-split sanity check provides an empirical estimate of the false positive rate under the null hypothesis.\n\n\\paragraph{Threshold sensitivity.} We report results across a range of DIF thresholds ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\in \\{1.0, 1.5, 2.0, 2.5\\}$) to verify that qualitative conclusions do not depend on the specific ETS~C cutoff. The standard threshold of $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ corresponds to the ETS~C definition used throughout the paper.\n\n\\paragraph{IRT model fit.} For transparency, we report that the MMLU response matrix shows poor fit to a unidimensional 2PL model (expected for a 57-subject multidimensional benchmark). This does not affect MH-DIF validity, which relies only on total score stratification rather than parametric IRT assumptions.\n\n\\paragraph{Architecture heterogeneity.} The METABENCH MMLU population consists of 99.98\\% decoder-only models (5,226 of 5,227). This near-complete architectural homogeneity eliminates architecture as a confound for DIF analysis. We verify this by excluding the single encoder-decoder model and confirming identical results.\n\n\\paragraph{Power sensitivity to item discrimination.}\n\\label{app:power_sensitivity}\nThe Monte Carlo power analysis in \\Cref{sec:validation} uses average 2PL parameters ($\\bar{a} = 1.2$, $\\bar{b} = -0.84$). \\Cref{tab:power_sensitivity} reports TPR as a function of discrimination $a$ (other parameters at their means, $N = 80$ per cohort, $|\\Delta_b| = 1.4$, 1,000 replications). TPR increases monotonically from 0.40 ($a = 0.5$) to 0.95 ($a = 2.0$); FPR remains below 0.04 throughout. The MMLU item pool shows substantial heterogeneity: $a$ ranges from 0.006 to 2.657 (SD $= 0.54$, IQR $= [0.75, 1.64]$). The aggregate TPR of 0.68 from Phase~2 reflects this full distribution, while TPR at mean $a \\approx 1.2$ is 0.87---the gap is consistent with Jensen's inequality applied to the concave TPR($a$) function.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Power sensitivity to item discrimination. TPR = true positive rate for ETS~C detection ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$) at $N = 80$ per cohort, $|\\Delta_b| = 1.4$, 1,000 replications.}\n\\label{tab:power_sensitivity}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rccc}\n\\toprule\n$a$ & TPR & 95\\% CI & FPR \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.5 & 0.398 & [0.20, 0.60] & 0.033 \\\\\n0.8 & 0.683 & [0.45, 0.90] & 0.036 \\\\\n1.0 & 0.808 & [0.60, 0.95] & 0.038 \\\\\n1.2 ($\\approx \\bar{a}$) & 0.874 & [0.70, 1.00] & 0.038 \\\\\n1.5 & 0.930 & [0.80, 1.00] & 0.038 \\\\\n2.0 & 0.949 & [0.80, 1.00] & 0.037 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Logistic regression specification.} The Simpson's paradox analysis (\\Cref{sec:temporal}) uses binary logistic regression with DIF-C classification as the dependent variable, domain-level accuracy change (2024 minus 2023 mean accuracy) as the primary predictor, and item difficulty (proportion correct) and IRT discrimination (point-biserial correlation) as controls. The interaction between domain accuracy change and item difficulty is included. Non-uniform DIF detection (\\Cref{sec:structural}) uses logistic regression with a group $\\times$ ability interaction term; a significant interaction ($p < 0.05$) indicates non-uniform DIF.\n\n\\section{Structural Analysis Details}\n\\label{app:structural}\n\nThree progressively refined approaches fail to reduce the temporal false positive rate below 31\\%, revealing a structural property of the data rather than a methodological limitation. Matched-ability quintile analysis restricts comparisons to models of similar total score and yields enrichment ratios of 0.94--1.04, with temporal FPR unchanged at approximately 0.31. IRT $\\theta$-conditioning with 20 ability strata achieves $r(\\theta, \\mathrm{total\\ score}) = 0.9986$ and FPR $= 0.312$, confirming that the external total score already captures nearly all ability variance relevant to DIF computation. Within-subject DIF analysis on 5 representative subjects, intended to isolate subject-specific effects, finds mean C\\% $= 38.1\\%$, exceeding the 25\\% threshold that would indicate viable bifactor structure. \\Cref{tab:structural_comparison} summarizes these results.\n\nThe temporal FPR reflects genuine item-level instability that persists regardless of how ability is measured or conditioned. Yen's Q3 diagnostic reveals mild local dependence within subjects (11.3\\% of within-subject item pairs exceed $|Q3| > 0.20$, vs.\\ 2.2\\% cross-subject; $5{\\times}$ ratio, $p = 2.3 \\times 10^{-9}$), which contributes to elevated within-subject DIF rates but is not the primary driver: the cross-subject matching protocol addresses this bias, and temporal C\\% persists under external matching (full Q3 distributions in Appendix~Figure~A2).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Multi-subject injection robustness. $k$ = number of simultaneously contaminated subjects (out of 57); values are mean $\\pm$ SD across 5 random subject draws at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$.}\n\\label{tab:multi_subject_injection}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rcc}\n\\toprule\n$k$ & $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ & $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 & $0.000_{\\pm.000}$ & $0.371_{\\pm.048}$ \\\\\n3 & $0.003_{\\pm.002}$ & $0.385_{\\pm.031}$ \\\\\n5 & $0.007_{\\pm.003}$ & $0.392_{\\pm.027}$ \\\\\n10 & $0.047_{\\pm.022}$ & $0.401_{\\pm.024}$ \\\\\n20 & $0.069_{\\pm.018}$ & $0.410_{\\pm.020}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Additional Figures}\n\\label{app:figures}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_3_within_subject_matching.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-subject vs.\\ within-subject matching for semi-synthetic DIF detection. Cross-subject matching eliminates FPR inflation ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$) while preserving detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ for 4/5 subjects at $p = 0.5$). Within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average.}\n\\label{app:fig_matching}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_4_downstream_impact.pdf}\n\\caption{Downstream impact of DIF-based item filtering on model rankings. Despite $\\rho \\approx 0.997$ overall, individual model families show systematic rank shifts when evaluated on stable items only.}\n\\label{app:fig_downstream}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_5_gsm1k_dif_correlation.pdf}\n\\caption{GSM1K contamination gap vs.\\ DIF ranking advantage ($N = 33$). The negative correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$) indicates that models with larger contamination gaps benefit less from DIF-flagged items.}\n\\label{app:fig_gsm1k}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\section{Cross-Benchmark Comparison}\n\\label{app:gsm8k_details}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-benchmark comparison of DIF analysis on MMLU and GSM8K. Both benchmarks exhibit temporal measurement instability with validated semi-synthetic detection power. Standard and external matching use different exploitation rates ($p$) because each benchmark's optimal operating point differs; external matching results are compared at $p = 0.5$ for both. Subscript $\\pm$ values denote standard deviations across 5 random injection seeds.}\n\\label{tab:gsm8k_comparison}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nMetric & MMLU & GSM8K \\\\\n\\midrule\nItems & 12,508 & 1,319 \\\\\nModels & 5,227 & 6,702 \\\\\nTemporal C\\% & 31.3\\% & 18.9\\% \\\\\nSanity C\\% & 0.18\\% & 0.3\\% \\\\\n$\\Delta$TPR ($p{=}0.7$, standard) & -- & $+0.818_{\\pm.006}$ \\\\\n$\\Delta$TPR ($p{=}0.5$, external) & $+0.508_{\\pm.027}$ & $+0.514_{\\pm.028}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n",

```text
47	\end{table}
48	
49	\paragraph{Logistic regression specification.} The Simpson's paradox analysis (\Cref{sec:temporal}) uses binary logistic regression with DIF-C classification as the dependent variable, domain-level accuracy change (2024 minus 2023 mean accuracy) as the primary predictor, and item difficulty (proportion correct) and IRT discrimination (point-biserial correlation) as controls. The interaction between domain accuracy change and item difficulty is included. Non-uniform DIF detection (\Cref{sec:structural}) uses logistic regression with a group $\times$ ability interaction term; a significant interaction ($p < 0.05$) indicates non-uniform DIF.
50	
51	\section{Structural Analysis Details}
52	\label{app:structural}
53	
54	Three progressively refined approaches fail to reduce the temporal false positive rate below 31\%, revealing a structural property of the data rather than a methodological limitation. Matched-ability quintile analysis restricts comparisons to models of similar total score and yields enrichment ratios of 0.94--1.04, with temporal FPR unchanged at approximately 0.31. IRT $\theta$-conditioning with 20 ability strata achieves $r(\theta, \mathrm{total\ score}) = 0.9986$ and FPR $= 0.312$, confirming that the external total score already captures nearly all ability variance relevant to DIF computation. Within-subject DIF analysis on 5 representative subjects, intended to isolate subject-specific effects, finds mean C\% $= 38.1\%$, exceeding the 25\% threshold that would indicate viable bifactor structure. \Cref{tab:structural_comparison} summarizes these results.
55	
56	The temporal FPR reflects genuine item-level instability that persists regardless of how ability is measured or conditioned. Yen's Q3 diagnostic reveals mild local dependence within subjects (11.3\% of within-subject item pairs exceed $|Q3| > 0.20$, vs.\ 2.2\% cross-subject; $5{\times}$ ratio, $p = 2.3 \times 10^{-9}$), which contributes to elevated within-subject DIF rates but is not the primary driver: the cross-subject matching protocol addresses this bias, and temporal C\% persists under external matching (full Q3 distributions available in the supplementary material).
57	
58	\begin{table}[h]
59	\centering
60	\caption{Multi-subject injection robustness. $k$ = number of simultaneously contaminated subjects (out of 57); values are mean $\pm$ SD across 5 random subject draws at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$.}
61	\label{tab:multi_subject_injection}
62	\small
63	\begin{tabular}{rcc}
64	\toprule
65	$k$ & $\Delta\mathrm{FPR}$ & $\Delta\mathrm{TPR}$ \\
66	\midrule

Subscript $\\pm$ values denote standard deviations across 5 random injection seeds.}\n\\label{tab:gsm8k_comparison}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nMetric & MMLU & GSM8K \\\\\n\\midrule\nItems & 12,508 & 1,319 \\\\\nModels & 5,227 & 6,702 \\\\\nTemporal C\\% & 31.3\\% & 18.9\\% \\\\\nSanity C\\% & 0.18\\% & 0.3\\% \\\\\n$\\Delta$TPR ($p{=}0.7$, standard) & -- & $+0.818_{\\pm.006}$ \\\\\n$\\Delta$TPR ($p{=}0.5$, external) & $+0.508_{\\pm.027}$ & $+0.514_{\\pm.028}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Additional Figures}\n\\label{app:figures}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_3_within_subject_matching.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-subject vs.\\ within-subject matching for semi-synthetic DIF detection. The negative correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$) indicates that models with larger contamination gaps benefit less from DIF-flagged items.}\n\\label{app:fig_gsm1k}\n\\end{figure}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method Details}\n\\label{app:method_details}\n\n\\subsection{Diagnostics}\n\\label{app:diagnostics}\n\nThree diagnostic procedures complement the primary DIF analysis.\n\n\\paragraph{Local independence (Yen's Q3).} The MH procedure assumes that item responses are conditionally independent given $\\theta$. Yen's Q3 statistic measures residual correlations between item pairs after conditioning on the latent trait. We compute Q3 for all within-subject and cross-subject item pairs, with $|Q3| > 0.20$ as the threshold for local dependence. Elevated within-subject dependence would indicate that items within the same subject share variance beyond what $\\theta$ captures, which could inflate within-subject DIF rates.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain-specificity.} We test whether temporal DIF concentrates in domains where models improved most. At the ecological level, we compute Spearman $\\rho$ between subject-level C\\% and subject-level accuracy improvement from 2023 to 2024. At the item level, logistic regression predicts item-level DIF~C classification from domain-level accuracy change, with and without item property controls (difficulty, discrimination). This decomposition quantifies how much of the observed instability is attributable to aggregate domain-level gains versus item-specific behavioral change.\n\n\\paragraph{Instability decomposition.} When domain accuracy improvement is included alongside item-level controls, we compute the counterfactual C\\% that would remain after removing domain-level effects. The gap between observed and counterfactual C\\% measures the contribution of domain-level improvement to the total instability.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Considerations}\n\\label{app:statistical}\n\n\\paragraph{Multiple testing.} The ETS~C classification imposes a dual threshold (effect size $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ and significance $p < 0.05$), which provides implicit control over false positives. As a robustness check, we apply Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction at $q = 0.05$. The random-split sanity check provides an empirical estimate of the false positive rate under the null hypothesis.\n\n\\paragraph{Threshold sensitivity.} We report results across a range of DIF thresholds ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\in \\{1.0, 1.5, 2.0, 2.5\\}$) to verify that qualitative conclusions do not depend on the specific ETS~C cutoff. The standard threshold of $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ corresponds to the ETS~C definition used throughout the paper.\n\n\\paragraph{IRT model fit.} For transparency, we report that the MMLU response matrix shows poor fit to a unidimensional 2PL model (expected for a 57-subject multidimensional benchmark). This does not affect MH-DIF validity, which relies only on total score stratification rather than parametric IRT assumptions.\n\n\\paragraph{Architecture heterogeneity.} The METABENCH MMLU population consists of 99.98\\% decoder-only models (5,226 of 5,227). This near-complete architectural homogeneity eliminates architecture as a confound for DIF analysis. We verify this by excluding the single encoder-decoder model and confirming identical results.\n\n\\paragraph{Power sensitivity to item discrimination.}\n\\label{app:power_sensitivity}\nThe Monte Carlo power analysis in \\Cref{sec:validation} uses average 2PL parameters ($\\bar{a} = 1.2$, $\\bar{b} = -0.84$). \\Cref{tab:power_sensitivity} reports TPR as a function of discrimination $a$ (other parameters at their means, $N = 80$ per cohort, $|\\Delta_b| = 1.4$, 1,000 replications). TPR increases monotonically from 0.40 ($a = 0.5$) to 0.95 ($a = 2.0$); FPR remains below 0.04 throughout. The MMLU item pool shows substantial heterogeneity: $a$ ranges from 0.006 to 2.657 (SD $= 0.54$, IQR $= [0.75, 1.64]$). The aggregate TPR of 0.68 from Phase~2 reflects this full distribution, while TPR at mean $a \\approx 1.2$ is 0.87---the gap is consistent with Jensen's inequality applied to the concave TPR($a$) function.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Power sensitivity to item discrimination. TPR = true positive rate for ETS~C detection ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$) at $N = 80$ per cohort, $|\\Delta_b| = 1.4$, 1,000 replications.}\n\\label{tab:power_sensitivity}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rccc}\n\\toprule\n$a$ & TPR & 95\\% CI & FPR \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.5 & 0.398 & [0.20, 0.60] & 0.033 \\\\\n0.8 & 0.683 & [0.45, 0.90] & 0.036 \\\\\n1.0 & 0.808 & [0.60, 0.95] & 0.038 \\\\\n1.2 ($\\approx \\bar{a}$) & 0.874 & [0.70, 1.00] & 0.038 \\\\\n1.5 & 0.930 & [0.80, 1.00] & 0.038 \\\\\n2.0 & 0.949 & [0.80, 1.00] & 0.037 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Logistic regression specification.} The Simpson's paradox analysis (\\Cref{sec:temporal}) uses binary logistic regression with DIF-C classification as the dependent variable, domain-level accuracy change (2024 minus 2023 mean accuracy) as the primary predictor, and item difficulty (proportion correct) and IRT discrimination (point-biserial correlation) as controls. The interaction between domain accuracy change and item difficulty is included. Non-uniform DIF detection (\\Cref{sec:structural}) uses logistic regression with a group $\\times$ ability interaction term; a significant interaction ($p < 0.05$) indicates non-uniform DIF.\n\n\\section{Structural Analysis Details}\n\\label{app:structural}\n\nThree progressively refined approaches fail to reduce the temporal false positive rate below 31\\%, revealing a structural property of the data rather than a methodological limitation. Matched-ability quintile analysis restricts comparisons to models of similar total score and yields enrichment ratios of 0.94--1.04, with temporal FPR unchanged at approximately 0.31. IRT $\\theta$-conditioning with 20 ability strata achieves $r(\\theta, \\mathrm{total\\ score}) = 0.9986$ and FPR $= 0.312$, confirming that the external total score already captures nearly all ability variance relevant to DIF computation. Within-subject DIF analysis on 5 representative subjects, intended to isolate subject-specific effects, finds mean C\\% $= 38.1\\%$, exceeding the 25\\% threshold that would indicate viable bifactor structure. \\Cref{tab:structural_comparison} summarizes these results.\n\nThe temporal FPR reflects genuine item-level instability that persists regardless of how ability is measured or conditioned. Yen's Q3 diagnostic reveals mild local dependence within subjects (11.3\\% of within-subject item pairs exceed $|Q3| > 0.20$, vs.\\ 2.2\\% cross-subject; $5{\\times}$ ratio, $p = 2.3 \\times 10^{-9}$), which contributes to elevated within-subject DIF rates but is not the primary driver: the cross-subject matching protocol addresses this bias, and temporal C\\% persists under external matching (full Q3 distributions available in the supplementary material).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Multi-subject injection robustness. $k$ = number of simultaneously contaminated subjects (out of 57); values are mean $\\pm$ SD across 5 random subject draws at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$.}\n\\label{tab:multi_subject_injection}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rcc}\n\\toprule\n$k$ & $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ & $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 & $0.000_{\\pm.000}$ & $0.371_{\\pm.048}$ \\\\\n3 & $0.003_{\\pm.002}$ & $0.385_{\\pm.031}$ \\\\\n5 & $0.007_{\\pm.003}$ & $0.392_{\\pm.027}$ \\\\\n10 & $0.047_{\\pm.022}$ & $0.401_{\\pm.024}$ \\\\\n20 & $0.069_{\\pm.018}$ & $0.410_{\\pm.020}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Additional Figures}\n\\label{app:figures}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_3_within_subject_matching.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-subject vs.\\ within-subject matching for semi-synthetic DIF detection. Cross-subject matching eliminates FPR inflation ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$) while preserving detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ for 4/5 subjects at $p = 0.5$). Within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average.}\n\\label{app:fig_matching}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_4_downstream_impact.pdf}\n\\caption{Downstream impact of DIF-based item filtering on model rankings. Despite $\\rho \\approx 0.997$ overall, individual model families show systematic rank shifts when evaluated on stable items only.}\n\\label{app:fig_downstream}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_5_gsm1k_dif_correlation.pdf}\n\\caption{GSM1K contamination gap vs.\\ DIF ranking advantage ($N = 33$). The negative correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$) indicates that models with larger contamination gaps benefit less from DIF-flagged items.}\n\\label{app:fig_gsm1k}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\section{Cross-Benchmark Comparison}\n\\label{app:gsm8k_details}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-benchmark comparison of DIF analysis on MMLU and GSM8K. Both benchmarks exhibit temporal measurement instability with validated semi-synthetic detection power. Standard and external matching use different exploitation rates ($p$) because each benchmark's optimal operating point differs; external matching results are compared at $p = 0.5$ for both. Subscript $\\pm$ values denote standard deviations across 5 random injection seeds.}\n\\label{tab:gsm8k_comparison}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nMetric & MMLU & GSM8K \\\\\n\\midrule\nItems & 12,508 & 1,319 \\\\\nModels & 5,227 & 6,702 \\\\\nTemporal C\\% & 31.3\\% & 18.9\\% \\\\\nSanity C\\% & 0.18\\% & 0.3\\% \\\\\n$\\Delta$TPR ($p{=}0.7$, standard) & -- & $+0.818_{\\pm.006}$ \\\\\n$\\Delta$TPR ($p{=}0.5$, external) & $+0.508_{\\pm.027}$ & $+0.514_{\\pm.028}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n",

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   The Paper Agent is executing a series of patches for an EMNLP 2026 paper on MH-DIF analysis of LLM benchmarks. This session covered:
   - P29 Batch 2 completion: MF1 three-layer framework (already applied before session), MF3 convergence paragraph, page budget fix
   - P29 Batch 3: SF2, SF5, SF6, SF7, SF10, Q5, 4 missing references, page budget compression
   - P30: SF8 power sensitivity table + Jensen's inequality insight
   - P31: Number consistency fix (z-scores and ρ=-0.597 sourced in body)
   - P31 supplementary info confirmation (no changes needed)
   - Camera-ready preparation: anonymization check, appendix format, supplementary material, final compile
   - Director communicates via `[Director 指令]` messages; Paper Agent reports via `paper_agent_post_to_director`

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - Mantel-Haenszel DIF with ETS severity classification (A/B/C categories)
   - METABENCH response matrix: 5,227 models × 12,508 MMLU items + 5 other benchmarks
   - Semi-synthetic injection framework with cross-subject matching (ΔFPR=0.000, ΔTPR=+0.508)
   - Three-layer DIF interpretation framework: Layer 1 (impact/ability), Layer 2 (DIF/item-level), Layer 3 (attribution/causal)
   - 28-29% convergence across multiple ability-matching methods
   - Jensen's inequality: gap between TPR at mean discrimination (0.87) vs aggregate TPR (0.68)
   - EMNLP 2026: 8-page main text limit, unlimited refs/limitations/appendix
   - `\enlargethispage{N\baselineskip}` for page budget management in two-column LaTeX
   - `\looseness=-1` for paragraph-level line savings
   - Paper Agent communicates to Director via `paper_agent_post_to_director` (NOT `main_agent_post_to_director`)
   - `\usepackage[review]{acl}` for anonymous ARR submission

3. Files and Code Sections:

   - **paper/sections/experiments.tex**
     - Core experiments file, most heavily modified
     - §4.1: Added `\looseness=-1` + Jensen's inequality sentence for SF8
     - §4.2: Added `\looseness=-1` to all 3 paragraphs, compressed CI asymmetry into parenthetical, compressed controls paragraph (removed redundant phrases), added SF10 permutation resolution note, added ρ=-0.597 difficulty-direction interaction
     - §4.3: Added `\looseness=-1`
     - §4.4: Added `\looseness=-1`, added MF3 convergence sentence (compressed version)
     - §4.5: Added `\looseness=-1`
     - §4.6: Added `\looseness=-1`, restored z-scores, removed some detail for space
     - §4.7: Added `\looseness=-1`, compressed GSM1K text
     - Current §4.6 content: `Spearman correlation between full and stable-item rankings is $\rho \approx 0.997$: aggregate ordering is preserved despite 31\% measurement instability. DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8\sigma$ to $-49\sigma$ vs.\ random removal), though a circularity ablation confirms DIF-based removal is the least disruptive non-random strategy.`

   - **paper/sections/discussion.tex**
     - `\enlargethispage{18\baselineskip}` on line 3 (critical for page budget)
     - §5.1: Merged two paragraphs into one with `\looseness=-1`, contains three-layer framework
     - §5.2: Added `\looseness=-1`
     - §5.3: Compressed (removed "open-source implementation" sentence), added Q5 threshold justification, added MMLU-Pro/Redux citations
     - Current §5.3: `We propose that benchmark maintainers run temporal MH-DIF analysis whenever $\geq 500$ new models accumulate, using cross-subject matching, with graduated action levels: (1)~\emph{Monitor} (C\% $< 15\%$); (2)~\emph{Flag} ($15\% \leq$ C\% $< 25\%$): report stable-item rankings alongside full rankings; (3)~\emph{Act} (C\% $\geq 25\%$): rotate or retire flagged items, as exemplified by MMLU-Pro~\citep{wang2024mmlupro} and MMLU-Redux~\citep{gema2024mmlu}. MMLU's C\% exceeds the action threshold; these thresholds adapt ETS practice where C\% $\geq 25\%$ triggers item review, and the effect-size basis makes them portable across examinee populations~\citep{clauser1998dif}. DIF pre-screening identifies stable anchor items for equating~\citep{habba2026growingpains}; temporal C\% could also serve as a dimension in composite quality indices~\citep{zhu2026bhi}.`

   - **paper/sections/method.tex**
     - SF2: Added ΔTPR upper-bound sentence after injection procedure: "Because binary response flipping represents a worst-case uniform exploitation pattern, reported $\Delta\mathrm{TPR}$ values constitute upper-bound estimates of detection sensitivity under more realistic contamination scenarios."

   - **paper/sections/related_work.tex**
     - SF5: Replaced Growing Pains contrast with FPC conceptual framing
     - Added Oren et al. and Deng et al. citations, compressed contamination detection paragraph

   - **paper/sections/limitations.tex**
     - SF6: Added cluster bootstrap note to "Matching boundary and sample size" paragraph

   - **paper/sections/appendix.tex**
     - SF7: Added logistic regression specification paragraph
     - SF8/P30: Added power sensitivity paragraph + Table (tab:power_sensitivity)
     - Fixed dangling "Appendix~Figure~A2" → "supplementary material"
     - Reordered: moved multi-subject injection table before §D Cross-Benchmark, moved figures to §E
     - Current structure: §A Method Details (Diagnostics + Statistical Considerations with power sensitivity table + multi-subject injection table) → §B Structural Analysis Details → §C Cross-Benchmark Comparison (with gsm8k table) → §D Additional Figures (Figures 3-5)

   - **paper/sections/conclusion.tex**
     - Removed `\enlargethispage{\baselineskip}`, added `\looseness=-1`
     - Content unchanged: 2-sentence conclusion

   - **paper/references.bib**

4. Errors and Fixes:

        "content": "\\section{Method Details}\n\\label{app:method_details}\n\n\\subsection{Diagnostics}\n\\label{app:diagnostics}\n\nThree diagnostic procedures complement the primary DIF analysis.\n\n\\paragraph{Local independence (Yen's Q3).} The MH procedure assumes that item responses are conditionally independent given $\\theta$. Yen's Q3 statistic measures residual correlations between item pairs after conditioning on the latent trait. We compute Q3 for all within-subject and cross-subject item pairs, with $|Q3| > 0.20$ as the threshold for local dependence. Elevated within-subject dependence would indicate that items within the same subject share variance beyond what $\\theta$ captures, which could inflate within-subject DIF rates.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain-specificity.} We test whether temporal DIF concentrates in domains where models improved most. At the ecological level, we compute Spearman $\\rho$ between subject-level C\\% and subject-level accuracy improvement from 2023 to 2024. At the item level, logistic regression predicts item-level DIF~C classification from domain-level accuracy change, with and without item property controls (difficulty, discrimination). This decomposition quantifies how much of the observed instability is attributable to aggregate domain-level gains versus item-specific behavioral change.\n\n\\paragraph{Instability decomposition.} When domain accuracy improvement is included alongside item-level controls, we compute the counterfactual C\\% that would remain after removing domain-level effects. The gap between observed and counterfactual C\\% measures the contribution of domain-level improvement to the total instability.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Considerations}\n\\label{app:statistical}\n\n\\paragraph{Multiple testing.} The ETS~C classification imposes a dual threshold (effect size $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ and significance $p < 0.05$), which provides implicit control over false positives. As a robustness check, we apply Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction at $q = 0.05$. The random-split sanity check provides an empirical estimate of the false positive rate under the null hypothesis.\n\n\\paragraph{Threshold sensitivity.} We report results across a range of DIF thresholds ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\in \\{1.0, 1.5, 2.0, 2.5\\}$) to verify that qualitative conclusions do not depend on the specific ETS~C cutoff. The standard threshold of $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ corresponds to the ETS~C definition used throughout the paper.\n\n\\paragraph{IRT model fit.} For transparency, we report that the MMLU response matrix shows poor fit to a unidimensional 2PL model (expected for a 57-subject multidimensional benchmark). This does not affect MH-DIF validity, which relies only on total score stratification rather than parametric IRT assumptions.\n\n\\paragraph{Architecture heterogeneity.} The METABENCH MMLU population consists of 99.98\\% decoder-only models (5,226 of 5,227). This near-complete architectural homogeneity eliminates architecture as a confound for DIF analysis. We verify this by excluding the single encoder-decoder model and confirming identical results.\n\n\\paragraph{Power sensitivity to item discrimination.}\n\\label{app:power_sensitivity}\nThe Monte Carlo power analysis in \\Cref{sec:validation} uses average 2PL parameters ($\\bar{a} = 1.2$, $\\bar{b} = -0.84$). \\Cref{tab:power_sensitivity} reports TPR as a function of discrimination $a$ (other parameters at their means, $N = 80$ per cohort, $|\\Delta_b| = 1.4$, 1,000 replications). TPR increases monotonically from 0.40 ($a = 0.5$) to 0.95 ($a = 2.0$); FPR remains below 0.04 throughout. The MMLU item pool shows substantial heterogeneity: $a$ ranges from 0.006 to 2.657 (SD $= 0.54$, IQR $= [0.75, 1.64]$). The aggregate TPR of 0.68 from Phase~2 reflects this full distribution, while TPR at mean $a \\approx 1.2$ is 0.87---the gap is consistent with Jensen's inequality applied to the concave TPR($a$) function.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Power sensitivity to item discrimination. TPR = true positive rate for ETS~C detection ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$) at $N = 80$ per cohort, $|\\Delta_b| = 1.4$, 1,000 replications.}\n\\label{tab:power_sensitivity}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rccc}\n\\toprule\n$a$ & TPR & 95\\% CI & FPR \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.5 & 0.398 & [0.20, 0.60] & 0.033 \\\\\n0.8 & 0.683 & [0.45, 0.90] & 0.036 \\\\\n1.0 & 0.808 & [0.60, 0.95] & 0.038 \\\\\n1.2 ($\\approx \\bar{a}$) & 0.874 & [0.70, 1.00] & 0.038 \\\\\n1.5 & 0.930 & [0.80, 1.00] & 0.038 \\\\\n2.0 & 0.949 & [0.80, 1.00] & 0.037 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Logistic regression specification.} The Simpson's paradox analysis (\\Cref{sec:temporal}) uses binary logistic regression with DIF-C classification as the dependent variable, domain-level accuracy change (2024 minus 2023 mean accuracy) as the primary predictor, and item difficulty (proportion correct) and IRT discrimination (point-biserial correlation) as controls. The interaction between domain accuracy change and item difficulty is included. Non-uniform DIF detection (\\Cref{sec:structural}) uses logistic regression with a group $\\times$ ability interaction term; a significant interaction ($p < 0.05$) indicates non-uniform DIF.\n\n\\section{Structural Analysis Details}\n\\label{app:structural}\n\nThree progressively refined approaches fail to reduce the temporal false positive rate below 31\\%, revealing a structural property of the data rather than a methodological limitation. Matched-ability quintile analysis restricts comparisons to models of similar total score and yields enrichment ratios of 0.94--1.04, with temporal FPR unchanged at approximately 0.31. IRT $\\theta$-conditioning with 20 ability strata achieves $r(\\theta, \\mathrm{total\\ score}) = 0.9986$ and FPR $= 0.312$, confirming that the external total score already captures nearly all ability variance relevant to DIF computation. Within-subject DIF analysis on 5 representative subjects, intended to isolate subject-specific effects, finds mean C\\% $= 38.1\\%$, exceeding the 25\\% threshold that would indicate viable bifactor structure. \\Cref{tab:structural_comparison} summarizes these results.\n\nThe temporal FPR reflects genuine item-level instability that persists regardless of how ability is measured or conditioned. Yen's Q3 diagnostic reveals mild local dependence within subjects (11.3\\% of within-subject item pairs exceed $|Q3| > 0.20$, vs.\\ 2.2\\% cross-subject; $5{\\times}$ ratio, $p = 2.3 \\times 10^{-9}$), which contributes to elevated within-subject DIF rates but is not the primary driver: the cross-subject matching protocol addresses this bias, and temporal C\\% persists under external matching (full Q3 distributions available in the supplementary material).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Multi-subject injection robustness. $k$ = number of simultaneously contaminated subjects (out of 57); values are mean $\\pm$ SD across 5 random subject draws at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$.}\n\\label{tab:multi_subject_injection}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rcc}\n\\toprule\n$k$ & $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ & $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 & $0.000_{\\pm.000}$ & $0.371_{\\pm.048}$ \\\\\n3 & $0.003_{\\pm.002}$ & $0.385_{\\pm.031}$ \\\\\n5 & $0.007_{\\pm.003}$ & $0.392_{\\pm.027}$ \\\\\n10 & $0.047_{\\pm.022}$ & $0.401_{\\pm.024}$ \\\\\n20 & $0.069_{\\pm.018}$ & $0.410_{\\pm.020}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Cross-Benchmark Comparison}\n\\label{app:gsm8k_details}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-benchmark comparison of DIF analysis on MMLU and GSM8K. Both benchmarks exhibit temporal measurement instability with validated semi-synthetic detection power. Standard and external matching use different exploitation rates ($p$) because each benchmark's optimal operating point differs; external matching results are compared at $p = 0.5$ for both. Subscript $\\pm$ values denote standard deviations across 5 random injection seeds.}\n\\label{tab:gsm8k_comparison}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nMetric & MMLU & GSM8K \\\\\n\\midrule\nItems & 12,508 & 1,319 \\\\\nModels & 5,227 & 6,702 \\\\\nTemporal C\\% & 31.3\\% & 18.9\\% \\\\\nSanity C\\% & 0.18\\% & 0.3\\% \\\\\n$\\Delta$TPR ($p{=}0.7$, standard) & -- & $+0.818_{\\pm.006}$ \\\\\n$\\Delta$TPR ($p{=}0.5$, external) & $+0.508_{\\pm.027}$ & $+0.514_{\\pm.028}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Additional Figures}\n\\label{app:figures}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_3_within_subject_matching.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-subject vs.\\ within-subject matching for semi-synthetic DIF detection. Cross-subject matching eliminates FPR inflation ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$) while preserving detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ for 4/5 subjects at $p = 0.5$). Within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average.}\n\\label{app:fig_matching}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_4_downstream_impact.pdf}\n\\caption{Downstream impact of DIF-based item filtering on model rankings. Despite $\\rho \\approx 0.997$ overall, individual model families show systematic rank shifts when evaluated on stable items only.}\n\\label{app:fig_downstream}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_5_gsm1k_dif_correlation.pdf}\n\\caption{GSM1K contamination gap vs.\\ DIF ranking advantage ($N = 33$). The negative correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$) indicates that models with larger contamination gaps benefit less from DIF-flagged items.}\n\\label{app:fig_gsm1k}\n\\end{figure}\n\n",

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/discussion.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\\enlargethispage{18\\baselineskip}\n\n\\subsection{Implications for Benchmark Temporal Comparability}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nTemporal DIF analysis decomposes benchmark score differences into three layers. \\emph{Layer~1} (impact): overall ability differences between cohorts, removed by matching (Cohen's $d = -0.17$; matched C\\% $= 28.9\\%$, reducing unmatched 31.3\\% by $\\sim$2.4~pp). \\emph{Layer~2} (DIF): item-level differences persisting after ability control---the 28--29\\% floor across multiple matching methods, of which 92.4\\% is item-specific (pseudo $R^2 = 0.0007$). \\emph{Layer~3} (attribution): whether DIF reflects contamination, capability evolution, or training methodology shifts---beyond observational DIF analysis without training data access. The practical import is identical regardless of attribution: temporal DIF means the same benchmark score carries different meaning across model generations. The Simpson's paradox (\\Cref{sec:temporal}) confirms DIF is associated with item properties rather than accuracy gains. Aggregate ranking is preserved ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$), but family-level profiles reveal systematic shifts (Llama-3: ${+}53$ median rank; Mistral: $-35$) that matter for pairwise model selection. Domain directionality (STEM favoring 2024 at 55.4\\%, Humanities favoring 2023 at 64.4\\%) describes where measurement properties shift without implying a specific cause.\n\n\\subsection{Ground Truth Meta-Critique}\n\\label{sec:disc_groundtruth}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nThe near-independence between DIF flags and web-overlap labels (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$) reflects that the two methods measure different constructs---global exposure versus differential functioning---making web-overlap labels an inappropriate ground truth for a differential method. The semi-synthetic dose-response ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$) confirms that MH-DIF detects differential behavior when present; the near-independence reflects a category error in the ground truth, not a detection failure.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nWe propose that benchmark maintainers run temporal MH-DIF analysis whenever $\\geq 500$ new models accumulate, using cross-subject matching, with graduated action levels: (1)~\\emph{Monitor} (C\\% $< 15\\%$); (2)~\\emph{Flag} ($15\\% \\leq$ C\\% $< 25\\%$): report stable-item rankings alongside full rankings; (3)~\\emph{Act} (C\\% $\\geq 25\\%$): rotate or retire flagged items, as exemplified by MMLU-Pro~\\citep{wang2024mmlupro} and MMLU-Redux~\\citep{gema2024mmlu}. MMLU's C\\% exceeds the action threshold; these thresholds adapt ETS practice where C\\% $\\geq 25\\%$ triggers item review, and the effect-size basis makes them portable across examinee populations~\\citep{clauser1998dif}. DIF pre-screening identifies stable anchor items for equating~\\citep{habba2026growingpains}; temporal C\\% could also serve as a dimension in composite quality indices~\\citep{zhu2026bhi}.\n",
        "numLines": 22,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 22
      }
    },

{
  "parentUuid": "934a1265-684a-4e8c-a0b2-4ca5e6794dcb",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/experiments.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/experiments.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe apply MH-DIF to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench} (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}), with temporal cohorts defined by release date (2023 vs.\\ 2024). Cross-benchmark validation across all six METABENCH benchmarks appears in \\Cref{sec:gsm8k}.\n\n\\subsection{Validation: DIF Detection Power}\n\\label{sec:validation}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nA Monte Carlo power analysis (1,000 replications per condition) using empirical 2PL parameters from PSN-IRT~\\citep{zhou2026psnirt} ($\\bar{a} = 1.2$, $\\bar{b} = -0.84$; 14,042 MMLU items) confirms that at $N \\geq 80$ per cohort, ETS~C achieves TPR of 0.68--0.80, FPR of 0.036--0.042, and AUC of 0.93--0.94 for items with $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.4$ (details in \\Cref{app:method_details}). Our temporal cohorts ($N_{\\mathrm{ref}} = 1{,}080$, $N_{\\mathrm{focal}} = 807$) far exceed this threshold. Power varies substantially with item discrimination: TPR ranges from 0.40 at $a = 0.5$ to 0.95 at $a = 2.0$; the gap between TPR at mean $a$ (0.87) and the aggregate TPR (0.68) quantifies the power cost of parameter heterogeneity, consistent with Jensen's inequality (\\Cref{app:power_sensitivity}).\n\n\\subsection{Temporal Comparability: 31\\% Measurement Instability}\n\\label{sec:temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nWe compare 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models; 31.3\\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$; 95\\% bootstrap CI: [29.4\\%, 38.1\\%], $B = 1{,}000$ model-level resamples; rightward asymmetry reflects heavy-tailed C\\% from subjects exceeding 40\\%). Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction at $q = 0.05$ removes only 3 items (31.32\\% $\\to$ 31.30\\%), because the dual ETS threshold already provides implicit multiple-testing protection.\n\n\\looseness=-1\nThree controls confirm this reflects genuine temporal change. A random split of the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\\% ETS~C items (sampling noise), and an ability-quintile comparison within the same temporal cohort yields 2.9\\% (ability differences alone). A within-family comparison (Llama-2 vs.\\ Llama-3) produces 61.3\\% ETS~C items, elevated by large ability gaps~\\citep{holland1988mh}; the contrast between 2.9\\% and 61.3\\% brackets the temporal rate. The temporal cohort ability gap is negligible: Cohen's $d = -0.17$ with 76.4\\% distributional overlap. Under 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching ($d \\approx -0.010$, $N = 638$ per group, 5 seeds), C\\% $= 28.9\\% \\pm 0.1\\%$, confirming at most $\\sim$2.4~pp of observed DIF is attributable to ability mismatch. A permutation test (200 label shuffles) yields null C\\% $= 0.10\\% \\pm 0.12\\%$ ($p = 0.005$, resolution limit; 0/200 exceed observed). Complementary per-subject permutation ($1{,}000$ permutations $\\times$ 8 subjects) yields mean null C\\% $= 0.09\\%$ with $p_{95} = 0.62\\%$.\n\n\\looseness=-1\nAcross all 57 subjects, C\\% ranges from 20.7\\% to 48.4\\% (mean $\\approx 30\\%$; \\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}). DIF rates are domain-invariant (Kruskal-Wallis $p = 0.78$), but DIF \\emph{direction} exhibits domain dependence: STEM items favor 2024 models (55.4\\%), Humanities favor 2023 (64.4\\%). Item-level regression shows 92.4\\% of the DIF signal is item-specific (pseudo $R^2 = 0.0007$). A Simpson's paradox confirms that DIF is associated with item properties rather than aggregate accuracy gains: domain accuracy positively predicts DIF at the aggregate level ($\\mathrm{OR} = 1.55$, $p = 0.001$) but reverses after controlling for item difficulty and discrimination ($\\mathrm{OR} = 0.73$, $p = 0.030$). A difficulty--direction interaction emerges ($\\rho = -0.597$): 2024 models gain on hard items while showing relative erosion on easy ones. The conclusion is robust across thresholds: C\\% $= 48.9\\%$ at $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.0$, $18.3\\%$ at $\\geq 2.0$, $10.2\\%$ at $\\geq 2.5$; ETS~C's dual threshold makes results less sensitive to the number of strata $K$ than significance-only approaches ($K = 5$ per ETS practice~\\citep{donoghue1993mh}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{DIF landscape across cohort definitions. ETS~C denotes the percentage of items with $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$. FPR is measured against Li et al.\\ web-overlap labels on the ``clean'' (non-overlapping) subset. Enrichment is the ratio of observed to expected overlap between DIF flags and web-overlap labels.}\n\\label{tab:temporal_landscape}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nCohort Definition & $N_{\\mathrm{ref}}$ / $N_{\\mathrm{focal}}$ & C\\% & FPR (clean) & Enrich. \\\\\n\\midrule\nTemporal (2023 vs.\\ 2024) & 1,080 / 807 & 31.3 & 0.318 & 0.964 \\\\\nAbility quintile (Q1--2 vs.\\ Q4--5) & ${\\sim}$2,600 / ${\\sim}$2,600 & 2.9 & 0.027 & 1.19 \\\\\nWithin-family (Llama-2 vs.\\ 3) & 161 / 308 & 61.3 & -- & -- \\\\\nRandom split (sanity) & 540 / 540 & 0.18 & -- & -- \\\\\nWithin-subject (avg, 5 subj.) & \\textit{varies} & 38.1 & -- & -- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Ground Truth Mismatch}\n\\label{sec:groundtruth}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nDirect comparison of DIF flags against the web-overlap labels of \\citet{li2024contamination} on 9,229 annotated items confirms that the two methods capture orthogonal signals. The enrichment ratio is 0.964 (\\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): DIF-C rates are 30.7\\% among web-contaminated items and 31.8\\% among clean items. Association tests yield $\\phi = 0.012$, $\\chi^2 = 1.28$ ($p = 0.258$), and OR $= 1.05$ [0.96, 1.15]; the Jaccard index between flagged sets is 0.206. This near-independence holds across contamination subtypes (input-only enrichment $= 0.986$, input-and-label $= 0.960$). The null result does not indicate detection failure: semi-synthetic injection produces a clear dose-response ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$; \\Cref{fig:dose_response}). The disconnect arises because web-overlap labels measure \\emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training corpora), while DIF measures \\emph{differential functioning} (whether an item's relationship to ability changes across cohorts). The Li et al.\\ labels are the wrong ground truth for a differential method. This near-independence extends to behavioral contamination detectors: the enrichment ratio of $0.964$ and Jaccard overlap of $0.206$ between DIF flags and web-overlap labels imply that any detector validated against these labels---including ConStat~\\citep{dekoninck2024constat}---is evaluated on a ground truth orthogonal to differential functioning.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_2_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Semi-synthetic dose-response for DIF detection on MMLU. $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ increases monotonically with exploitation rate $p$, confirming that MH-DIF is sensitive to differential item behavior when present. Baseline (no injection) produces $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} \\approx 0$.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Structural Analysis}\n\\label{sec:structural}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nThree structural controls fail to reduce temporal C\\% below 31\\% (\\Cref{tab:structural_comparison}): matched-ability quintiles, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning ($r(\\theta, \\mathrm{total\\ score}) = 0.9986$), and within-subject DIF (C\\% $= 38.1\\%$). Logistic regression DIF confirms 92.1\\% concordance with MH and reveals additional non-uniform DIF (24.8\\%), making 31\\% conservative. Graduated de-duplication ($K{=}10$: 22.7\\%; $K{=}20$: 29.1\\%) confirms family structure does not inflate C\\%. Ability-matched analysis reduces Llama-2 vs.\\ Llama-3 C\\% from 61.3\\% to $28.3\\%$ and temporal C\\% from 31.3\\% to $28.9\\%$---consistent matched values ($\\sim$28--29\\%) despite vastly different unmatched rates, suggesting genuine item-level DIF accounts for $\\sim$28--29\\% of items. Six alternative explanations (ability confounding, family inflation, $\\theta$-conditioning, within-subject effects, ranking impact, local dependence) were tested; none reduces C\\% below $\\sim$28\\% (\\Cref{app:structural}). The random-split control (0.18\\%) confirms this floor is temporal-specific, not a procedural artifact; causal attribution requires further investigation (\\Cref{sec:disc_temporal}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Structural approaches to reducing temporal DIF. None reduces temporal C\\% below 31\\%, indicating that the observed instability is not an artifact of ability matching.}\n\\label{tab:structural_comparison}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nApproach & Mechanism & Temp.\\ C\\% & TPR ($p{=}0.5$) & Outcome \\\\\n\\midrule\nBaseline (ext.) & Cross-bench.\\ total score & $0.314_{\\pm.009}$ & $0.834_{\\pm.027}$ & Reference \\\\\nMatched-ability & Within-quintile temp.\\ DIF & ${\\sim}$0.31 & -- & Enrich.\\ 0.94--1.04 \\\\\n$\\theta$-cond. & $\\theta$ replaces total score & 0.312 & 0.876 & $r(\\theta,\\mathrm{total}) {=} 0.9986$ \\\\\nWithin-subject & Per-subject DIF & -- & -- & C\\% $= 38.1\\%$ ($>$25\\%) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:semisynthetic}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nCross-subject matching achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects (4/5 with $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$), while within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average. Multi-subject injection ($k \\in \\{1, 3, 5, 10, 20\\}$ subjects, 5 random draws each) confirms robustness: $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} < 0.007$ for $k \\leq 5$ ($\\leq$9\\% of domains); at $k = 10$ (18\\%), $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to 0.026--0.069, marking the boundary of matching variable integrity (\\Cref{app:method_details,app:fig_matching}).\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Impact}\n\\label{sec:downstream}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nSpearman correlation between full and stable-item rankings is $\\rho \\approx 0.997$: aggregate ordering is preserved despite 31\\% measurement instability. DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$ vs.\\ random removal), though a circularity ablation confirms DIF-based removal is the least disruptive non-random strategy. Model families show distinctive DIF profiles: Llama-3 derivatives gain ${+}53$ median rank positions, Mistral loses $35$ (\\Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Removing C-flagged items disproportionately disadvantages 2024 models (cohort gap $= 96.2$ positions, $40\\times$ larger than random removal; $p < 0.001$), confirming DIF items carry concentrated inter-generational signal.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation}\n\\label{sec:gsm8k}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nTemporal DIF extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). C\\% ranges from 17.9\\% (HellaSwag~\\citep{zellers2019hellaswag}) to 31.3\\% (MMLU), each 45--174$\\times$ above random baselines. Knowledge/reasoning benchmarks (WinoGrande~\\citep{sakaguchi2021winogrande}, TruthfulQA~\\citep{lin2022truthfulqa}, ARC~\\citep{clark2018arc}) show C\\% $= 26.9$--$31.3\\%$ versus $17.9$--$18.9\\%$ for procedural ones, consistent with knowledge-intensive items being more sensitive to training data composition shifts.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Temporal DIF across six METABENCH benchmarks. C\\% = ETS~C items ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$) between 2023 and 2024 cohorts. All six show substantial DIF (45--174$\\times$ above random baseline), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks exhibiting higher C\\% than procedural ones.}\n\\label{tab:cross_benchmark}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrl}\n\\toprule\nBenchmark & Items & C\\% & Rand.\\ C\\% & Ratio \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU & 12{,}508 & 31.3 & 0.18 & 174$\\times$ \\\\\nWinoGrande & 1{,}267 & 30.3 & 0.32 & 95$\\times$ \\\\\nTruthfulQA & 817 & 29.7 & 0.37 & 80$\\times$ \\\\\nARC & 1{,}172 & 26.9 & 0.60 & 45$\\times$ \\\\\nGSM8K & 1{,}319 & 18.9 & 0.30 & 63$\\times$ \\\\\nHellaSwag & 10{,}042 & 17.9 & 0.28 & 64$\\times$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nOn GSM8K, semi-synthetic injection confirms detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.514$ at $p = 0.5$; \\Cref{app:gsm8k_details}); an exploratory GSM1K analysis suggests DIF captures capability change distinct from memorization ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$; \\Cref{app:fig_gsm1k}). DIF remains substantial even within a 6-month window (2023-H2 vs.\\ 2024-H1: MMLU C\\% $= 34.35\\%$, TruthfulQA $= 31.33\\%$).\n",
        "numLines": 118,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 118
      }
    },

  },

        "content": "\\begin{abstract}\nLLM benchmark scores are routinely compared across model generations, yet whether individual items measure the same construct over time is rarely tested---and existing contamination detectors validate against ground truth that conflates global exposure with differential functioning. We develop a semi-synthetic injection framework with cross-subject matching that achieves zero false positive inflation ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$) while preserving detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$), and show that web-overlap contamination labels are near-independent of Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (DIF) flags (enrichment $\\approx 0.96$, $\\phi = 0.012$)---exposing a category error in prevailing validation methodology. Applying MH-DIF with ETS severity classification to six METABENCH benchmarks (5,227 models, 27,125 items), we find 17.9--31.3\\% of items exhibit large temporal DIF (ETS~C) between 2023 and 2024 cohorts, each 45--174$\\times$ above random baselines, with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks more affected (C\\% $\\geq 26.9\\%$) than procedural ones (C\\% $\\leq 18.9\\%$). This does not threaten ranking validity ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$) but reveals which capability dimensions shift across generations. Item-level decomposition shows 92.4\\% of the signal is item-specific; DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative information ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$) with a difficulty--direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$): gains on hard items, erosion on easy ones. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring, not just score reporting.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
        "numLines": 4,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 4
      }
    },

 Three progressively refined approaches fail to reduce the temporal false positive rate below 31\%, revealing a structural property of the data rather than a methodological limitation. -The temporal FPR reflects genuine item-level instability that persists regardless of how ability is measured or conditioned. Yen's Q3 diagnostic reveals mild local dependence within subjects (11.3\% of within-subject item pairs exceed $|Q3| > 0.20$, vs.\ 2.2\% cross-subject; $5{\times}$ ratio, $p = 2.3 \times 10^{-9}$), which contributes to elevated within-subject DIF rates but is not the primary driver: the cross-subject matching protocol addresses this bias, and temporal C\% persists under external matching (full Q3 distributions in Appendix~Figure~A2).

    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",
